\langle O\rangle_{\text{ideal}}:=\text{Tr}[Oe^{\mathcal{L}t}(\rho(0))],

and the expected value on the quantum simulator after evolving it for time
t/\omega^{2}
,

\displaystyle\langle O\rangle_{\text{sim}}=\text{Tr}\bigg[O\rho_{\omega}\bigg(\frac{t}{\omega^{2}}\bigg)\bigg]=\text{Tr}\bigg[O\text{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}\bigg({\rho_{\omega}\bigg(\frac{t}{\omega^{2}}\bigg)\bigg)}\bigg].

(15)

Using the integral form of the remainder definition, Eq.
12
, we can express the error between
\langle O\rangle_{\text{ideal}}
and
\langle O\rangle_{\text{sim}}
as

\displaystyle{\left|\langle O\rangle_{\text{ideal}}-\langle O\rangle_{\text{sim}}\right|}={\left|\int_{0}^{t/\omega^{2}}\text{Tr}\bigg(Oe^{\mathcal{L}(t-\omega^{2}s)}\mathcal{R}_{\omega}(s)\bigg)ds\right|},

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{\omega^{2}}{\left|\int_{0}^{t}\text{Tr}\bigg(O(t-s)\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}\bigg(\mathcal{R}_{\omega}\bigg(\frac{s}{\omega^{2}}\bigg)\bigg)\bigg)ds\right|},

(16)

where
O(t)=e^{\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}t}(O)
is the Heisenberg picture evolution of the observable
O
under the
target
Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}
. Using Lemma
1
, we express the remainder
\mathcal{R}_{\omega}(s)
in terms of the operators
q_{\alpha}
,
\mathcal{Q}^{(1)}_{\alpha,H_{\textnormal{sys}}}(s)
,
\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{\alpha,H_{\textnormal{sys}}}(s)
,
\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(s)
, and
\mathcal{Q}^{(4)}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(s)
and analyze the resulting terms individually. The next lemma shows that applying the Lieb-Robinson bounds in Lemmas
3
and
4
provides upper bounds on these remainder terms that are uniform in the system size.

Lemma 5
.

Suppose
O
is a local observable with
{|\hskip-1.084pt|O|\hskip-1.084pt|}\leq 1
supported on
S_{O}
, and for
\tau>0
, let
O(\tau)=\exp(\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}\tau)(O)
where
\mathcal{L}
is a geometrically local Lindbladian of the form in Eq.
14
. Then for
q_{\alpha}
as defined in Lemma
1
, then there is a non-decreasing piecewise continuous function
\nu
such that
\nu(t)\leq O\left(t^{d}\right)
as
t\to\infty
and

\sum_{\alpha}{\left|\textnormal{Tr}(O(\tau)q_{\alpha})\right|}\leq\nu(\tau),

and for
j\in\{1,2,3,4\}

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}{\left|\textnormal{Tr}(O(\tau)\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}^{(j)}(s))\right|}\leq\nu^{2}(\tau),

where, for
j\in\{3,4\}
,
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}^{(j)}
is defined in Lemma
1
and for
j\in\{1,2\}
, we define
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}^{(j)}=\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,h_{\alpha^{\prime}}}^{(j)}
where
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,h}^{(j)}
is defined in Lemma
1
.

This lemma follows directly from the application of the Lieb-Robinson bounds. The proof of this lemma is detailed in Appendix
B
, but we provide a sketch here. Consider first
\sum_{\alpha}{|\text{Tr}(O(\tau)q_{\alpha}|}
— using the explicit expression for
q_{\alpha}
from Lemma
1
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha}{|\text{Tr}(O(\tau)q_{\alpha}|}=\sum_{\alpha}{|\text{Tr}[O(\tau)\mathcal{L}_{\alpha}(\rho(0))]|},

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{\alpha}{\left|\text{Tr}[\mathcal{L}_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(O(\tau))\rho(0)]\right|},

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{\alpha}{{|\hskip-1.084pt|\mathcal{L}_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(O(\tau))|\hskip-1.084pt|}}.

(17)

Now, we can use the Lieb-Robinson bound in Lemma
3
, together with
{|\hskip-1.084pt|\mathcal{L}_{\alpha}^{\dagger}|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{cb,\infty\to\infty}\leq 4
to obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha}{|\text{Tr}(O(\tau)q_{\alpha}|}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 4\sum_{\alpha}\min\bigg(\eta_{S_{O}}\exp\bigg(4e\mathcal{Z}\tau-\frac{d(S_{\alpha},S_{O})}{a}\bigg),1\bigg).

(18)

However, the summation in the above equation is simply a summation over an exponential over the lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, which will be convergent and independent of the size of system. A careful analysis of this summation, performed in Appendix
B
, yields that, as stated in the lemma, it can be upper bounded by a non-decreasing function which is
O(\tau^{d})
. Similarly, as an example, consider
\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\text{Tr}(O(\tau)\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(s))
— by using the explicit expression for
\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(s)
from Lemma
1
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}{\left|\text{Tr}(O(\tau)\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(s))\right|}

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{\omega}\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{u\in\{-,+\}}{\left|\text{Tr}\big(O(\tau)\mathcal{D}_{L_{\alpha}}([L_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{(u)},\text{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{(\bar{u})}\rho_{\omega}(s))])\big)\right|},

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{\omega}\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{u\in\{-,+\}}{\left|\text{Tr}\big([\mathcal{D}_{L_{\alpha}}^{\dagger}(O(\tau)),L_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{(u)}]\text{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{(\bar{u})}\rho_{\omega}(s))\big)\right|},

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{1}{\omega}\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{u\in\{-,+\}}{|\hskip-1.084pt|[\mathcal{D}_{L_{\alpha}}^{\dagger}(O(\tau)),L_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{(u)}]|\hskip-1.084pt|}{|\hskip-1.084pt|\text{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{(\bar{u})}\rho_{\omega}(s))|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{1},

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{u\in\{-,+\}}{|\hskip-1.084pt|[\mathcal{D}_{L_{\alpha}}^{\dagger}(O(\tau)),L_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{(u)}]|\hskip-1.084pt|},

where in the last step we have used Lemma
2
. We can note that both
\mathcal{D}_{L_{\alpha}}^{\dagger}
and
[\cdot,L_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{(u)}]
are superoperators that are respectively supported on
S_{\alpha}
and
S_{\alpha^{\prime}}
and have the identity operator in their kernel. Thus from Lemma
4
, as well as using the fact that
{|\hskip-1.084pt|\mathcal{D}_{L_{\alpha}}^{\dagger}|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{cb,\infty\to\infty},{|\hskip-1.084pt|[L_{\alpha}^{(u)},\cdot]|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{cb,\infty\to\infty}\leq 2
we obtain that

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}{\left|\text{Tr}(O(\tau)\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(s))\right|}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\min\bigg(e\eta_{S_{O}}\exp\bigg(4e\mathcal{Z}\tau-

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\frac{1}{2a}\big(d(S_{\alpha},S_{O})+d(S_{\alpha^{\prime}},S_{O})\big)\bigg),1\bigg).

This summation, which is approximately a double summation of a two-dimensional exponential function on a lattice, can again be analyzed and upper bounded by a non-decreasing function that grows as
O(\tau^{2d})
. The rest of the Lemma
5
can be proved similarly — the proofs are outlined in detail in Appendix
B
.

Proposition
2
, repeated
.

Suppose
\mathcal{L}
is a
d-
dimensional geometrically local Lindbladian and
O
with
{|\hskip-1.084pt|O|\hskip-1.084pt|}\leq 1
is a local observable. To achieve an additive error
\varepsilon
in the expected local observable at time
t
with the analogue quantum simulator, we need to choose
\omega=\Theta(t^{-(2d+1)}\sqrt{\varepsilon})
which corresponds to a simulator evolution time
t_{\textnormal{sim}}=t/\omega^{2}=\Theta(t^{4d+3}\varepsilon^{-1})
.

Proof.

The proposition follows by combining the expression of
\mathcal{R}_{\omega}(s)
from Lemma
1
with Eq.
12
and then using Lemma
5
. Using
H_{\text{sys}}=\sum_{\alpha}h_{\alpha}
, we first decompose
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,H_{\text{sys}}}^{(j)}=\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}}\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,h_{\alpha^{\prime}}}^{(j)}
for
j\in\{1,2\}
, where
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,h}^{(j)}
is defined in Lemma
1
. For notational convenience, for
j\in\{1,2\}
we will define
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,{\alpha^{\prime}}}^{(j)}=\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,h_{\alpha^{\prime}}}^{(j)}
. From Eq.
1
and Lemma
1
, it follows that

\displaystyle{|\langle O\rangle_{\text{ideal}}-\langle O\rangle_{\text{sim}}|}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\alpha}{\left|\int_{0}^{t}{\text{Tr}(O(t-s)q_{\alpha})}e^{-2s/\omega^{2}}ds\right|}+\omega^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{4}\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}{\left|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s/\omega^{2}}e^{-2(s/\omega^{2}-s^{\prime})}{\text{Tr}(O(t-s)\mathcal{Q}^{(j)}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(s^{\prime}))}ds^{\prime}\right|},

(19)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle(\mkern-1.5mu1\mkern-1.5mu)}}{{\leq}}\int_{0}^{t}\nu(t-s)e^{-2s/\omega^{2}}ds+4\omega^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s/\omega^{2}}e^{-2(s/\omega^{2}-s^{\prime})}\nu^{2}(t-s)ds^{\prime},

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle(\mkern-1.5mu2\mkern-1.5mu)}}{{\leq}}\nu(t)\int_{0}^{t}e^{-2s/\omega^{2}}ds+4\omega^{2}\nu^{2}(t)\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s/\omega^{2}}e^{-2(s/\omega^{2}-s^{\prime})}ds^{\prime}\leq\frac{\omega^{2}}{2}\nu(t)+2\omega^{2}t\nu^{2}(t),

where in (1) we have used Lemma
5
— in particular, the fact that
\nu(t)
is a piecewise continuous function and thus can be integrated to give a legitimate upper bound. In (2), we have used the fact that the function
\nu(t)
, introduced in Lemma
5
, is an non-decreasing function. Noting, again from Lemma
5
, that
\nu(t)\leq O(t^{d})
, we obtain that
{|\langle O\rangle_{\text{ideal}}-\langle O\rangle_{\text{sim}}|}\leq\omega^{2}O(t^{2d+1})
— consequently, to attain a precision
\varepsilon
in the simulated observable, we need to choose
\omega=\Theta(t^{-(d+1/2)}\varepsilon^{1/2})
which corresponds to a quantum simulation time of
t_{\text{sim}}=\Theta(t^{2d+1}\varepsilon^{-1})

∎

IV.2.2
Long-time dynamics and fixed point

Next, we consider the problem of simulating long-time dynamics and fixed point values of a local observable that is rapidly mixing for given a spatially local Lindbladian. As outlined in Section
II.1
, a local observable in a spatially local Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}
, with a unique fixed point
\sigma
, is considered to be rapidly mixing if it satisfies, for any initial state
\rho(0)
,

\displaystyle{|\text{Tr}(Oe^{\mathcal{L}t}(\rho(0))-\text{Tr}(O\sigma)|}\leq k({|S_{O}|},\gamma)e^{-\gamma t},

where
k(l,\gamma)
is a function that is
\text{poly}(l)
for a fixed
\gamma
and
\text{exp}(O(\gamma^{-\kappa}))
for a fixed
l
. Since the rapid mixing condition is satisfied for any initial state
\rho(0)
, it can equivalently be reformulated in the Heisenberg picture

\displaystyle{|\hskip-1.084pt|O(t)-\text{Tr}(O\sigma)I|\hskip-1.084pt|}\leq k({|S_{O}|},\gamma)e^{-\gamma t},

(20)

where
O(t)=e^{\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}t}(O)
is the Heisenberg-picture observable corresponding to
O
. For the problem of simulating such rapidly mixing observables at time
t
in geometrically local Lindbladians, we show that the
\omega
required is uniform in both system size and time. This also allows us to use the quantum simulator to approximate the fixed point expectation value of the local observable.

To establish this result, we start from Eq.
19
for the observable error, analyze it term by term and provide a bound uniform in
t
. The key lemma that we establish, which is the counterpart of Lemma
5
for dynamics, is the following

Lemma 6
.

Suppose
O
is a local observable with
{|\hskip-1.084pt|O|\hskip-1.084pt|}\leq 1
supported on
S_{O}
, and for
\tau>0
, let
O(\tau)=\exp(\mathcal{L}^{\textnormal{\dagger}}\tau)(O)
where
\mathcal{L}
is a geometrically local Lindbladian of the form in Eq.
14
. Furthermore, suppose
O
is rapidly mixing with respect to
\mathcal{L}
and satisfies Eq.
20
with
k({|S_{O}|},\gamma)\leq O(\exp(\gamma^{-\kappa}))
as
\gamma\to 0
. Then for
q_{\alpha}
as defined in Lemma
1
,

\sum_{\alpha}{\left|\int_{0}^{t}\textnormal{Tr}(O(t-s)q_{\alpha})e^{-2s/\omega^{2}}ds\right|}\leq\omega^{2}\lambda^{(1)}(\gamma),

where
\lambda^{(1)}(\gamma)\leq O(\gamma^{-d(\kappa+1)})
as
\gamma\to 0
; and for
j\in\{1,2,3,4\}

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}{\left|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s/\omega^{2}}\textnormal{Tr}(O(t-s)\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}^{(j)}(s^{\prime}))e^{-2(s/\omega^{2}-s^{\prime})}ds^{\prime}ds\right|}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\leq\lambda^{(2)}(\gamma),

where
\lambda^{(2)}(\gamma)\leq O(\gamma^{-(2d+1)(\kappa+1)})
as
\gamma\to 0
and for
j\in\{3,4\}
,
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}^{(j)}
is defined in Lemma
1
, and for
j\in\{1,2\}
, we define
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}^{(j)}=\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,h_{\alpha^{\prime}}}^{(j)}
where
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,h}^{(j)}
is defined in Lemma
1
.

Importantly, this lemma establishes bounds on the error terms appearing in Eq.
19
that are uniform in the time
t
— this is a consequence of not only the spatial locality of the target Lindbladian, but also an explicit accounting of the rapid mixing of the target observable (Eq.
20
). We provide a detailed proof of this lemma in Appendix
B
— below, we illustrate how we obtain bounds uniform in
t
. The key idea is instead of using the Lieb-Robinson bounds to obtain an upper bound on the observable error for
any
time, we use it for only short times and use the rapid mixing property (Eq.
20
) to upper bound the observable error for long times. For instance, consider upper bounding

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha}{\left|\int_{0}^{t}\text{Tr}(O(t-s)q_{\alpha})e^{-2s/\omega^{2}}ds\right|}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\sum_{\alpha}{\left|\int_{0}^{t}\text{Tr}(O(s)q_{\alpha})e^{-2(t-s)/\omega^{2}}ds\right|}.

(21)

Suppose
t_{\alpha}
is a time cutoff for separating long times from short times, which we will choose later and possibly allow it to vary with
\alpha
. We then split the integral with respect to time in Eq.
21
into a short time integral
e_{\alpha}^{\leq}(t,t_{\alpha})
and a long time integral
e_{\alpha}^{\geq}(t,t_{\alpha})
i.e.

\displaystyle{\left|\int_{0}^{t}\text{Tr}(O(s)q_{\alpha})e^{-2(t-s)/\omega^{2}}ds\right|}\leq e_{\alpha}^{\leq}(t,t_{\alpha})+e_{\alpha}^{\geq}(t,t_{\alpha}),

where

\displaystyle e_{\alpha}^{\leq}(t,t_{\alpha})={\left|\int_{0}^{\min(t,t_{\alpha})}\text{Tr}(O(s)q_{\alpha})e^{-2(t-s)/\omega^{2}}ds\right|}\text{ and}

\displaystyle e_{\alpha}^{\geq}(t,t_{\alpha})={\left|\int_{\min(t,t_{\alpha})}^{t}\text{Tr}(O(s)q_{\alpha})e^{-2(t-s)/\omega^{2}}ds\right|}.

The short-time integral,
e_{\alpha}^{\leq}(t,t_{\alpha})
, can be analyzed similar to the case of dynamics — more specifically, proceeding similar to the analyses in Eqs.
17
and
18
, we obtain that

\displaystyle e_{\alpha}^{\leq}(t,t_{\alpha})

\displaystyle\quad\leq 2\omega^{2}\min\bigg(\eta_{S_{O}}\exp\bigg(4e\mathcal{Z}\min(t,t_{\alpha})-\frac{d(S_{\alpha},S_{O})}{a}\bigg),1\bigg),

\displaystyle\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle(\mkern-1.5mu1\mkern-1.5mu)}}{{\leq}}2\omega^{2}\min\bigg(\eta_{S_{O}}\exp\bigg(4e\mathcal{Z}t_{\alpha}-\frac{d(S_{\alpha},S_{O})}{a}\bigg),1\bigg).

(22)

where in (1) we have used the fact that
\min(\eta_{S_{O}}\exp(4e\mathcal{Z}s-{d(S_{\alpha},S_{O})}/{a}),1)
is a non-decreasing function of
s
.

Next, for bounding the long-time integral,
e^{\geq}_{\alpha}(t,t_{\alpha})
, we explicitly use the rapid-mixing property of
O
(Eq.
20
) — in particular, we note that for

\displaystyle{|\text{Tr}(O(s)q_{\alpha})|}

\displaystyle\quad={|\text{Tr}((O(s)-\text{Tr}(O\sigma)I)q_{\alpha})+\text{Tr}(O\sigma)\text{Tr}(q_{\alpha})|},

\displaystyle\quad\leq{|\text{Tr}((O(s)-\text{Tr}(O\sigma)I)q_{\alpha})|}+{|\text{Tr}(q_{\alpha})|}{|\text{Tr}(O\sigma)|},

\displaystyle\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle(\mkern-1.5mu1\mkern-1.5mu)}}{{\leq}}{|\hskip-1.084pt|O(s)-\text{Tr}(O\sigma)I|\hskip-1.084pt|}{|\hskip-1.084pt|q_{\alpha}|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{1},

\displaystyle\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle(\mkern-1.5mu2\mkern-1.5mu)}}{{\leq}}4k({|S_{O}|},\gamma)e^{-\gamma s},

(23)

where in (1) we have used the expression for
q_{\alpha}
from Lemma
1
to obtain
\text{Tr}(q_{\alpha})=\text{Tr}(\mathcal{L}_{\alpha}(\rho(0)))=0
and in (2) we have used the fact that, by assumption,
O
is a rapid mixing observable and hence satisfies Eq.
4
. Now, note that if
t\leq t_{\alpha}
(or
\min(t,t_{\alpha})=t
),

e^{\geq}_{\alpha}(t,t_{\alpha})=0.

On the other hand, if
t\geq t_{\alpha}
(or
\min(t,t_{\alpha})=t_{\alpha}
),

\displaystyle e^{\geq}_{\alpha}(t,t_{\alpha})

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t_{\alpha}}^{t}{|\text{Tr}(O(s)q_{\alpha})|}e^{-2(t-s)/\omega^{2}}ds,

\displaystyle\leq 4k({|S_{O}|},\gamma)\int_{t_{\alpha}}^{t}e^{-\gamma s}e^{-2(t-s)/\omega^{2}}ds,

\displaystyle\leq 4k({|S_{O}|},\gamma)e^{-\gamma t_{\alpha}}\int_{t_{\alpha}}^{t}e^{-2(t-s)/\omega^{2}}ds,

\displaystyle\leq 2\omega^{2}k({|S_{O}|},\gamma)e^{-\gamma t_{\alpha}}.

(24)

Furthermore, it is also true that for
t\geq t_{\alpha}
,

\displaystyle e^{\geq}_{\alpha}(t,t_{\alpha})

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t_{\alpha}}^{t}{|\text{Tr}(O(s)q_{\alpha})|}e^{-2(t-s)/\omega^{2}}ds,

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t_{\alpha}}^{t}{|\hskip-1.084pt|O|\hskip-1.084pt|}{|\hskip-1.084pt|q_{\alpha}|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{1}e^{-2(t-s)/\omega^{2}}ds\leq 2\omega^{2}.

(25)

Combining Eqs.
23
,
24
and
25
, we obtain that for any
t,t_{\alpha}
,

\displaystyle e_{\alpha}^{\geq}(t,t_{\alpha})\leq 2\omega^{2}\min(k({|S_{O}|},\gamma)e^{-\gamma t_{\alpha}},1).

(26)

We note already that the bounds on both the short-time integral (Eq.
22
) and the long-time integral (Eq.
26
) are independent of the time
t
, but instead depend on the time cutoff
t_{\alpha}
that separate long times from short times. We can now choose
t_{\alpha}
in such as a way that
\sum_{\alpha}e^{\leq}_{\alpha}(t,t_{\alpha})
and
\sum_{\alpha}e^{\geq}_{\alpha}(t,t_{\alpha})
are uniform in system size. A specific choice for
t_{\alpha}
is to choose them to be

t_{\alpha}=\frac{1}{8e\mathcal{Z}a}d(S_{\alpha},S_{O}),

i.e. choose the time-cutoff to grow with distance of
S_{\alpha}
from the observable. With this choice, we note that

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha}e^{\leq}_{\alpha}(t,t_{\alpha})\leq 2\omega^{2}\sum_{\alpha}\min\bigg(\eta_{S_{O}}\exp\bigg(-\frac{d(S_{\alpha},S_{O})}{2a}\bigg),1\bigg),

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha}e^{\geq}_{\alpha}(t,t_{\alpha})

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 2\omega^{2}\sum_{\alpha}\min\bigg(k({|S_{O}|},\gamma)\exp\bigg(-\frac{\gamma d(S_{\alpha},S_{O})}{8e\mathcal{Z}a}\bigg),1\bigg),

both of which, since they are sums of a decaying exponential function on a lattice, converge and are independent of the system size. As is shown in Appendix
B
, the other error terms can be analyzed similarly. Furthermore, a more careful analysis of these summations allow us to also calculate their scaling with the parameter
\gamma
to obtain the exact upper bounds quoted in Lemma
6
.

With Lemma
6
, we can now establish the following proposition.

Proposition
3
, repeated
.

Suppose
\mathcal{L}
is a
d-
dimensional geometrically local Lindbladian and
O
with
{|\hskip-1.084pt|O|\hskip-1.084pt|}\leq 1
is a local observable supported on
O(1)
lattice sites satisfying rapid mixing (Eq.
4
). To achieve an additive error
\varepsilon
in the expected local observable at time
t
with the analogue quantum simulator, we need to choose

\omega=\Theta\left(\gamma^{(d+1/2)(\kappa+1)}\sqrt{\varepsilon}\right)

which corresponds to a simulator evolution time

t_{\textnormal{sim}}=t/\omega^{2}=\Theta\left(t\gamma^{-(\kappa+1)(2d+1)}\varepsilon^{-1}\right).

Proof.

This proposition can now be proved by directly combining Lemma
6
and Eq.
19
— in particular, we obtain that for any time
t
,

{|\langle O\rangle_{\text{ideal}}-\langle O\rangle_{\text{sim}}|}\leq\omega^{2}\left(\lambda^{(1)}(\gamma)+4\lambda^{(2)}(\gamma)\right).

In the limit of
\gamma\to 0
, we obtain that
{|\langle O\rangle_{\text{ideal}}-\langle O\rangle_{\text{sim}}|}\leq\omega^{2}O\left(\gamma^{-(2d+1)(\kappa+1)}\right)
. Thus, to obtain a precision
\varepsilon
in the simulated observable i.e. to achieve
{|\langle O\rangle_{\text{ideal}}-\langle O\rangle_{\text{sim}}|}\leq\varepsilon
, we need to choose
\omega=\Theta\left(\varepsilon^{1/2}\gamma^{(d+1/2)(\kappa+1)}\right)
.
∎

IV.3
Quantum advantage over classical algorithms

As discussed in the previous subsection, for the problems of simulating local observables in constant time dynamics for spatially local Lindbladians as well as in long-time dynamics and fixed points for rapidly mixing Lindbladians, an analogue quantum simulator can be very efficient, even obtaining a run-time that is independent of the system size. It is a natural question to ask if these problems are hard to solve on a classical computer, so that we can expect a quantum advantage with the analogue quantum simulation method discussed so far. In this section, we establish that, subject to the complexity assumption of BQP
\neq
BPP, we indeed expect superpolynomial advantage with a quantum simulator applied to both of these problems.

Figure 6:
Circuit architecture of the circuit being encoded into the geometrically local Lindbladian. In each round, the unitaries are applied in sequence, first on the first qubit, then on qubits 2 and 3, then on qubits 3 and 4 and so on.

The separation between the runtime of the analogue quantum simulator and classical algorithms is proven based on a technique to encode a quantum circuit on
N
qubits and of depth
T=\text{poly}(N)
into the fixed point of a geometrically local Lindbladian in two dimensions. This problem has been previously addressed for 5-local (but not geometrically local) Lindbladians
[
47
]
. A related line of work encoded a quantum circuit on
N
qubits and of depth
\text{poly}(N)
into the ground state of geometrically local Hamiltonians, starting with Hamiltonians in two dimensions
[
48
,
54
]
, followed by Hamiltonians even in one dimension
[
55
]
. We build upon the techniques developed in Ref.
[
47
]
for quantum circuit to 5-local Lindbladian encoding, and in Ref.
[
48
]
for quantum circuit to 2D spatially local Hamiltonian encoding and encode a quantum circuit into the fixed point of a 2D spatially local Lindbladian. Furthermore, one of our key technical contributions is to establish that the resulting Lindbladian has a mixing time of at most
\text{poly}(N)
, where
N
is the number of qubits in the circuit, and that its depth is
\text{poly}(N)
. More precisely, we consider circuits of architecture shown in Fig.
6
and establish that

Lemma 7
.

Suppose we are given a quantum circuit
\mathcal{C}
on
N
qubits with architecture shown in Fig.
6
(a) and with
R
rounds of gates. Then, there exists a two-dimensional spatially local Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}
on a
N\times R
grid of 6-level qudits with a unqiue fixed point
\sigma
, as well as a local observable
O
such that

\textnormal{Tr}(O\sigma)=\frac{1}{2NR}z_{\mathcal{C}},

where
z_{\mathcal{C}}
is the expected value of a pauli-
{Z}
operator on the last qubit at the output of
\mathcal{C}
. Furthermore, for any initial state
\rho(0)
of the two-dimensional grid of qudits,

{|\hskip-1.084pt|e^{\mathcal{L}t}(\rho(0))-\sigma|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{1}\leq c_{0}(N,R)\exp(-\gamma_{0}(N,R)t),

where
c_{0}(N,R)=O(N^{6}R^{2}\exp(O(NR)))
and
\gamma_{0}(N,R)=\Theta(N^{-3}R^{-3})
.

The circuit is described in terms of rounds instead of layers due to the nature of the circuit-to-2D-lindbladian encoding scheme which we describe in Appendix
C
. However we point out that this circuit architecture (Fig.
6
) is not restrictive — any quantum circuit on
N
qubits and depth
\text{poly}(N)
can be expressed in this format with at most
\text{poly}(N)
rounds. Furthermore, we also note that, while the Lindbladian in Lemma
7
is on
NR
qudits, it can trivially be embedded into the center of an infinite grid of qudits where the qudits other than these
NR
qudits are uncoupled and only experience a single qudit Lindbladian with a unique fixed point [Fig.
6
(b)]. The observable
O
in Lemma
7
will then have a well-defined thermodynamic limit and
N,R
will effectively become parameters of the Lindbladian on which the observable
O
depends instead of being the system size.

For quantum circuits with
R=\text{poly}(N)
rounds, Lemma
7
shows that it takes time
t=O(\text{poly}(N)\log(\varepsilon^{-1}))
for the encoding Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}
to converge
\varepsilon-
close to its fixed point
\sigma
. Then, measuring the local observable
O
to a precision of
1/(2NR)=1/\text{poly}(N)
effectively measures the
Z
operator on the last qubit at the output of the quantum circuit. Since for any decision problem in the BQP complexity class, measuring the
Z
operator in the last qubit is the only required measurement for solving the problem, Lemma
7
indicates that being able to take a local observable in the fixed point of any spatially local two-dimensional Lindbladian, even restricting to the class of Lindbladians that converge to the fixed point in time that scales at most polynomially in the system size, is a sufficient resource to perform an arbitrary quantum computation. The construction of this Lindbladian closely follows the circuit-to-Hamiltonian ground state mapping presented in Ref.
[
48
]
. We detail this construction, the calculation of its fixed point, as well as analysis of the convergence rate of the Lindbladian to its fixed point in Appendix
C
. Based on this lemma, we obtain the following proposition.

Proposition
4
, repeated
.

There cannot exist a classical algorithm that can, for every geometrically local 2D Lindbladian and a corresponding rapidly mixing local observable, compute the fixed point expected value of the local observable to additive error
\varepsilon
in time
\textnormal{poly}(\gamma^{-1},\varepsilon^{-1})
, unless
BQP = BPP
.

Proof.

Assume the contrary i.e. that there is a randomized classical algorithm that can obtain the fixed point expectation of a rapidly mixing local observable to an additive error
\varepsilon
in time
\textnormal{poly}(\gamma^{-1},\varepsilon^{-1})
. In such case, any decision problem
\in
BQP could be solved with this algorithm. To see this, note that the decision problem parameterized by the problem size
m
could be solved by measuring one qubit at the output of a quantum circuit on
N=\textnormal{poly}(m)
qubits with
R=\textnormal{poly}(m)
rounds. By Lemma
7
, the expectation value of the pauli-
Z
operator on the output qubit in such a quantum circuit can then be embedded into the fixed point expectation value of a rapidly mixing local observable with
\gamma^{-1}=\text{poly}(m)
. Furthermore, as given by Lemma
7
, the expected of value of this observable is
1/(2NR)\leq O(1/\text{poly}(m))
times smaller than the expected value of the pauli-
Z
operator, and thus needs to be computed to an
O(1/\text{poly}(m))
precision to effectively simulate the encoded circuit. Since this can be done with a randomized
\text{poly}(\gamma^{-1},\varepsilon^{-1})
classical algorithm in
\text{poly}(m)
time, we contradict the complexity assumption of
\text{BQP}\neq\text{BPP}
.
∎

V
Stability to errors

V.1
Stability analysis

Next, we consider the impact of noise on the quantum simulator. We will consider the model of the noisy quantum simulator given in Eq.
5
— the quantum simulator Lindbladian, in the presence of errors, will be given by

\mathcal{L}_{\omega,\delta}=\mathcal{L}_{\omega}+\delta\sum_{\beta}\mathcal{N}_{\beta},

where
\mathcal{N}_{\beta}
is a Lindbladian acting on the system qudits and/or the ancillae in a geometrically local region
S_{\beta}^{\prime}
. We will assume that the subset
S_{\beta}^{\prime}
at most intersects with
\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}
other subsets
S^{\prime}_{\beta}
or
S_{\alpha}
, where
S_{\alpha}
are the subsets of system qudits on which the target Lindbladian is defined (i.e. Eq.
14
). More specifically, for all
\beta
,

{|\{\beta^{\prime}:S^{\prime}_{\beta}\cap S_{\beta^{\prime}}^{\prime}\neq\emptyset\}|}+{|\{\alpha:S^{\prime}_{\beta}\cap S_{\alpha}\neq\emptyset\}|}\leq\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}.

In the remainder of this subsection, we will carefully analyze the error incurred in using the noisy quantum simulator. Our goal would be to show that, despite the hardware error rate
\delta\neq 0
, the quantum simulator can still obtain local observables to a precision that depends only on the hardware error rate and not on the system size.

Our analysis of the stability relies strongly on the extension of Lemmas
1
and
2
to the noisy setting i.e. where the quantum simulator dynamics are described by the Lindbladian in Eq.
5
. In particular, as is shown in Appendix
D
, Lemma
1
can be modified to obtain an upper bound on the remainder,
\mathcal{R}_{\omega,\delta}(t)
, corresponding to the time evolution of the noisy quantum simulator i.e.

\mathcal{R}_{\omega,\delta}(t)=\frac{d}{dt}\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\rho_{\omega,\delta}(t))-\omega^{2}\mathcal{L}\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\rho_{\omega,\delta}(t)).

More specifically, we obtain that

Lemma 8
.

If the ancillae are initially in
\ket{0}
then

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\omega,\delta}(t)=

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\omega}(t)+\delta\sum_{\beta}\mathcal{K}^{(0)}_{\beta}(t)

\displaystyle+\delta\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\omega^{j}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-2(t-s)}\mathcal{K}^{(j)}_{\alpha,\beta}(s)ds,

where
\mathcal{R}_{\omega}(t)
is as defined in Lemma
1
but with
\rho_{\omega}\to\rho_{\omega,\delta}
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{(0)}(t)

\displaystyle=\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{N}_{\beta}(\rho_{\omega,\delta}(t)),

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,\beta}^{(1)}(t)

\displaystyle=-i[L_{\alpha}^{\dagger},\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\alpha}\mathcal{N}_{\beta}(\rho_{\omega,\delta}))]+\textnormal{h.c.},

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,\beta}^{(2)}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}[L_{\alpha}^{\dagger},L_{\alpha}\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{N}_{\beta}(\rho_{\omega,\delta}(t))]+\textnormal{h.c.}.

Another key ingredient in the stability analysis is the following modification of Lemma
2
, whose proof is presented in Appendix
D
.

Lemma 9
.

Suppose
\rho_{\omega,\delta}(t)
is the joint state of the system and the ancilla qubits with the ancilla qubits initially being in the state
\ket{0}
, then for all
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle{|\hskip-1.084pt|\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\alpha}\rho_{\omega,\delta}(t))|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{1}\leq\frac{\omega}{2}+\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\delta\text{ and },

\displaystyle{|\hskip-1.084pt|\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\alpha}\sigma_{\alpha^{\prime}}\rho_{\omega,\delta}(t))|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{1},{|\hskip-1.084pt|\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\alpha}^{\dagger}\sigma_{\alpha^{\prime}}\rho_{\omega,\delta}(t))|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{1}\leq

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\frac{\omega^{2}}{4}+{\frac{\omega\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\delta}{2}}+\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\delta.

It should be noted that on setting the hardware noise
\delta
to 0, the bounds in Lemma
9
reproduce the bounds in the noiseless case obtained in Lemma
2
.

Dynamics
. Next, we can analyze the modified remainder provided in Lemma
8
term by term and explicitly use the Lieb-Robinson bounds (Lemmas
3
and
4
) to obtain bounds that are uniform in the system size. We first establish an extension of Lemma
5
to the noisy setting.

Lemma 10
.

Suppose
O
is a local observable on the system qudits with
{|\hskip-1.084pt|O|\hskip-1.084pt|}\leq 1
supported on
S_{O}
, and for
\tau>0
, let
O(\tau)=\exp(\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}\tau)(O)
where
\mathcal{L}
is a geometrically local target Lindbladian of the form in Eq.
14
. Then for
q_{\alpha}
as defined in Lemma
1
, then there are non-decreasing piecewise continuous function
\nu,\nu^{\prime}
such that
\nu(t),\nu^{\prime}(t)\leq O(t^{d})
as
t\to\infty
and for
\omega\leq 2

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha}{\left|\textnormal{Tr}(O(\tau)q_{\alpha})\right|}\leq\nu(\tau)\text{ and},

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}{\left|\textnormal{Tr}(O(\tau)\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}^{(j)}(s))\right|}\leq\bigg(1+\frac{2\delta\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}}{\omega}+\frac{4\delta\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}}{\omega^{2}}\bigg)\nu^{2}(\tau).

where, for
j\in\{3,4\}
,
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}^{(j)}
is defined in Lemma
1
with
\rho_{\omega}\to\rho_{\omega,\delta}
and for
j\in\{1,2\}
, we define
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}^{(j)}=\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,h_{\alpha^{\prime}}}^{(j)}
where
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,h}^{(j)}
is defined in Lemma
1
with
\rho_{\omega}\to\rho_{\omega,\delta}
. Furthermore,

\displaystyle\sum_{\beta}{\left|\textnormal{Tr}(O(\tau)\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{(0)}(s)\right|}\leq\nu^{\prime}(\tau)\text{ and },

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\beta}{\left|\textnormal{Tr}(O(\tau)\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,\beta}^{(j)}(s)\right|}\leq(\nu^{\prime}(\tau))^{2},

where
\mathcal{K}^{(0)}_{\beta}
and
\mathcal{K}^{(j)}_{\alpha,\beta}
for
j\in\{1,2\}
are defined in Lemma
8
.

In this lemma, the bounds on
\sum_{\alpha}{|\textnormal{Tr}(O(\tau)q_{\alpha})|}
and
\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}{|\textnormal{Tr}(O(\tau)\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}^{(j)}(s))|}
can be obtained by closely following the proof of Lemma
5
, but with an application of Lemma
9
instead of
2
. To obtain
\sum_{\beta}{|\text{Tr}(O(\tau)\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{(0)}(s)|}
, we note that since
O(\tau)=\exp(\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}\tau)(O)
is an operator that acts entirely on the system qudits,

\displaystyle{|\text{Tr}(O(\tau)\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{(0)}(s)|}

\displaystyle\qquad={|\text{Tr}((O(\tau)\otimes I_{\mathcal{A}})\mathcal{N}_{\beta}(\rho_{\omega,\delta}(s))|},

\displaystyle\qquad={|\text{Tr}(\mathcal{N}_{\beta}^{\dagger}(O(\tau)\otimes I_{\mathcal{A}})\rho_{\omega,\delta}(s))|},

\displaystyle\qquad\leq{|\hskip-1.084pt|\mathcal{N}_{\beta}^{\dagger}(O(\tau)\otimes I_{\mathcal{A}})|\hskip-1.084pt|}={|\hskip-1.084pt|\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\beta}^{\dagger}(O(\tau))|\hskip-1.084pt|},

where
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\beta}^{\dagger}
, defined by
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\beta}^{\dagger}(X)=\mathcal{N}_{\beta}(X\otimes I_{\mathcal{A}})
, is a superoperator acting entirely on the system qudits. Although
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\beta}^{\dagger}
isn’t necessarily the adjoint of a Lindbladian superoperator (like
\mathcal{N}_{\beta}
), it has the identity (on the system qudits) in its kernel i.e.
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\beta}^{\dagger}(I)=0
. Furthermore, since
\mathcal{N}_{\beta}^{\dagger}
is supported on
S^{\prime}_{\beta}
,
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\beta}^{\dagger}
is supported only on the system qudits contained in
S^{\prime}_{\beta}
, which we denote by
\tilde{S}^{\prime}_{\beta}
. Now, with an application of the Lieb-Robinson bounds (Lemma
3
), we obtain that

\displaystyle{|\text{Tr}(O(\tau)\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{(0)}(s)|}\leq\min\bigg(\eta_{S_{O}}\exp\bigg(4e\mathcal{Z}\tau-\frac{d(S_{O},\tilde{S}^{\prime}_{\beta})}{a}\bigg),1\bigg),

where we have used that
{|\hskip-1.084pt|\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\beta}^{\dagger}|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{cb,\infty\to\infty}\leq{|\hskip-1.084pt|\mathcal{N}_{\beta}^{\dagger}|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{cb,\infty\to\infty}\leq 1
. This bound immediately yields an upper bound on
\sum_{\beta}{|\text{Tr}(O(\tau)\mathcal{K}^{(0)}_{\beta}(s)|}
which is uniform in the system size, since it reduces it to the summation of a decaying exponential on a lattice. An analysis of the scaling of this upper bound with
\tau
, together with similar bounds on
\sum_{\alpha,\beta}{|\text{Tr}(O(\tau)\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,\beta}^{(j)}(s)|}
is provided in Appendix
D
.

Proposition
5
, repeated
.

Suppose
\mathcal{L}
is a
d-
dimensional geometrically local Lindbladian and
O
with
{|\hskip-1.084pt|O|\hskip-1.084pt|}\leq 1
is a local observable. Then, in the presence of noise with noise rate
\delta
, the expected local observable with the analogue quantum simulator can be obtained to a precision
\varepsilon=O(\delta^{1/2}t^{2d+1})
. Furthermore, to obtain this precision, we need to choose
\omega=\Theta(\delta^{1/4})
which results in a simulator run-time
t_{\textnormal{sim}}=t/\omega^{2}=\Theta(t\delta^{-1/2})
.

Proof.

Given a local observable, the error between the target expectation value
\langle O\rangle_{\text{target}}
and the expectation value obtained on a noisy simulator,
\langle O\rangle_{\text{sim}}
can be bounded by Eq.
16
, with the modified remainder
\mathcal{R}_{\omega,\delta}
from Lemma
8
instead of
\mathcal{R}_{\omega}
:

\displaystyle{|\langle O\rangle_{\text{target}}-\langle O\rangle_{\text{sim}}|}=\frac{1}{\omega^{2}}{\left|\int_{0}^{t}\text{Tr}\bigg(O(t-s)\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}\bigg(\mathcal{R}_{\omega,\delta}\bigg(\frac{s}{\omega^{2}}\bigg)\bigg)\bigg)ds\right|},

\displaystyle\quad\leq\sum_{\alpha}{\left|\int_{0}^{t}\text{Tr}(O(t-s)q_{\alpha})e^{-2s/\omega^{2}}ds\right|}+\omega^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{4}\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}{\left|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s/\omega^{2}}e^{-2(s/\omega^{2}-s^{\prime})}\text{Tr}(O(t-s)\mathcal{Q}^{(j)}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(s^{\prime}))ds^{\prime}ds\right|}+

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\frac{\delta}{\omega^{2}}\sum_{\beta}{\left|\int_{0}^{t}\text{Tr}\bigg(O(t-s)\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{(0)}\bigg(\frac{s}{\omega^{2}}\bigg)\bigg)ds\right|}+\frac{\delta}{\omega^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\sum_{\alpha,\beta}\omega^{j}{\left|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s/\omega^{2}}e^{-2(s/\omega^{2}-s^{\prime})}\textnormal{Tr}(O(t-s)\mathcal{K}^{(j)}_{\alpha,\beta}(s^{\prime}))ds^{\prime}ds\right|},

(27)

\displaystyle\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle(\mkern-1.5mu1\mkern-1.5mu)}}{{\leq}}\int_{0}^{t}\nu(t-s)e^{-2s/\omega^{2}}ds+4\omega^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s/\omega^{2}}\bigg(1+\frac{2\delta\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}}{\omega}+\frac{4\delta\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}}{\omega^{2}}\bigg)e^{-2(s/\omega^{2}-s^{\prime})}\nu^{2}(t-s)ds^{\prime}ds+

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\frac{\delta}{\omega^{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\nu^{\prime}(t-s)ds+\frac{\delta}{\omega^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\omega^{j}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s/\omega^{2}}e^{-2(s/\omega^{2}-s^{\prime})}{\nu^{\prime}}^{2}(t-s)ds^{\prime}ds,

\displaystyle\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle(\mkern-1.5mu2\mkern-1.5mu)}}{{\leq}}\nu(t)\int_{0}^{t}e^{-2s/\omega^{2}}ds+\frac{\delta}{\omega^{2}}\nu^{\prime}(t)\int_{0}^{t}ds+\bigg(4(\omega^{2}+2\omega\delta\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}+4\delta\mathcal{Z}^{\prime})\nu^{2}(t)+\frac{\delta}{\omega}(1+\omega){\nu^{\prime}}^{2}(t)\bigg)\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s/\omega^{2}}e^{-2(s/\omega^{2}-s^{\prime})}ds^{\prime}ds,

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{1}{2}\omega^{2}\nu(t)+\frac{t}{2}\bigg(4(\omega^{2}+2\omega\delta\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}+4\delta\mathcal{Z}^{\prime})\nu^{2}(t)+\frac{\delta}{\omega}(1+\omega){\nu^{\prime}}^{2}(t)+\frac{2\delta}{\omega^{2}}\nu^{\prime}(t)\bigg),

where in (1) we have used Lemma
10
and in (2), we have used the fact that the functions
\nu(t),\nu^{\prime}(t)
appearing in Lemma
10
are non-decreasing in
t
. From this bound, which is uniform in the system size
n
, it can be seen that, for a fixed
t
, a choice of
\omega
scaling as
\Theta(\delta^{1/4})
minimizes the error
{|\langle O\rangle_{\text{target}}-\langle O\rangle_{\text{sim}}|}
as
\delta\to 0
. Furthermore, with this choice of
\omega
, we obtain an observable error of
O(\delta^{1/2}t^{2d+1})
, as
\delta\to 0
and
t\to\infty
, independent of the system size.
∎

Long-time dynamics and fixed point
. Next, we consider rapidly mixing observables (Eq.
20
) and analyze stability of these observables for long-time dynamics or fixed points. For this, we will establish the following modification of Lemma
6
to the account for noise in the quantum simulator.

Lemma 11
.

Suppose
O
is a local observable with
{|\hskip-1.084pt|O|\hskip-1.084pt|}\leq 1
supported on
S_{O}
, and for
\tau>0
, let
O(\tau)=\exp(\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}\tau)(O)
where
\mathcal{L}
is a geometrically local Lindbladian of the form in Eq.
14
. Furthermore, suppose
O
is rapidly mixing with respect to
\mathcal{L}
and satisfies Eq.
20
with
k({|S_{O}|},\gamma)\leq O(\exp(\gamma^{-\kappa}))
. Then for
q_{\alpha}
as defined in Lemma
1
,

\sum_{\alpha}{\left|\int_{0}^{t}\textnormal{Tr}(O(t-s)q_{\alpha})e^{-2s/\omega^{2}}ds\right|}\leq\omega^{2}\lambda^{(1)}(\gamma),

where
\lambda^{(1)}(\gamma)\leq O(\gamma^{-d(\kappa+1)})
as
\gamma\to 0
and for
j\in\{1,2,3,4\}

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}{\left|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s/\omega^{2}}\textnormal{Tr}(O(t-s)\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}^{(j)}(s^{\prime}))e^{-2(s/\omega^{2}-s^{\prime})}ds^{\prime}ds\right|}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq\bigg(1+\frac{2\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\delta}{\omega}+\frac{4\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\delta}{\omega^{2}}\bigg)\lambda^{(2)}(\gamma),

where
\lambda^{(2)}(\gamma)\leq O(\gamma^{-(2d+1)(\kappa+1)})
as
\gamma\to 0
and for
j\in\{3,4\}
,
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}^{(j)}
is defined in Lemma
1
but with
\rho_{\omega}\to\rho_{\omega,\delta}
and for
j\in\{1,2\}
, we define
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}^{(j)}=\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,h_{\alpha^{\prime}}}^{(j)}
where
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,h}^{(j)}
is defined in Lemma
1
but with
\rho_{\omega}\to\rho_{\omega,\delta}
. Furthermore,

\displaystyle\sum_{\beta}{\left|\int_{0}^{t}\textnormal{Tr}\bigg(O(t-s)\mathcal{K}_{\beta}^{(0)}\bigg(\frac{s}{\omega^{2}}\bigg)\bigg)ds\right|}\leq{\lambda^{\prime}}^{(1)}(\gamma)

where
{\lambda^{\prime}}^{(1)}(\gamma)\leq O(\gamma^{-(d+1)(\kappa+1)})
as
\gamma\to 0
and for
j\in\{1,2\}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha,\beta}{\left|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s/\omega^{2}}\textnormal{Tr}\big(O(t-s)\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,\beta}^{(j)}(s^{\prime})\big)e^{-2(s/\omega^{2}-s^{\prime})}ds^{\prime}ds\right|}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq{\lambda^{\prime}}^{(2)}(\gamma),

where
{\lambda^{\prime}}^{(2)}(\gamma)\leq O(\gamma^{-(2d+1)(\kappa+1)})
as
\gamma\to 0
.

The proof of this lemma closely follows the same strategy as the proof of Lemma
6
for the noiseless case and is detailed in Appendix
D
. With this lemma, we can then establish the stability of the quantum simulator while simulating the long-time dynamics or fixed point expectation values of rapidly mixing observables.

Proposition
6
, repeated
.

Suppose
\mathcal{L}
is a
d-
dimensional geometrically local Lindbladian and
O
with
{|\hskip-1.084pt|O|\hskip-1.084pt|}\leq 1
is a local observable supported on
O(1)
lattice sites satisfying rapid mixing (Eq.
4
). Then, in the presence of noise with noise rate
\delta
, the expected local observable at any time
t
can be obtained to a precision
\varepsilon=O(\delta^{1/2}\gamma^{-(\kappa+1)(2d+1)})
, independent of
n
and
t
, with the analogue quantum simulator. Furthermore, to obtain this precision, we need to choose
\omega=\Theta(\delta^{1/4})
which results in a simulator run-time
t_{\textnormal{sim}}=t/\omega^{2}=O(t\delta^{-1/2})
.

Proof.

To bound the observable error, we start from Eq.
27
and use Lemma
11
— we then obtain

\displaystyle{|\langle O\rangle_{\text{target}}-\langle O\rangle_{\text{sim}}|}\leq

\displaystyle\qquad\omega^{2}\lambda^{(1)}(\gamma)+4(\omega^{2}+2\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\omega\delta+4\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}\delta)\lambda^{(2)}(\gamma)+

\displaystyle\qquad\frac{\delta}{\omega^{2}}{\lambda^{\prime}}^{(1)}(\gamma)+\frac{\delta}{\omega}(1+\omega){\lambda^{\prime}}^{(2)}(\gamma).

From this bound, which is uniform in the system size
n
, as well as the scalings of
\lambda^{(j)}(\gamma),{\lambda^{\prime}}^{(j)}(\gamma)
from Lemma
11
it can be seen that, for a fixed
\gamma
, a choice of
\omega
scaling as
\Theta(\delta^{1/4})
minimizes the error
{|\langle O\rangle_{\text{target}}-\langle O\rangle_{\text{sim}}|}
as
\delta\to 0
. Furthermore, with this choice of
\omega
, we obtain an observable error of
O(\delta^{1/2}\gamma^{-(\kappa+1)(2d+1)})
, as
\delta\to 0
and
\gamma\to 0
, independent of the system size.
∎

V.2
Quantum advantage in the presence of noise

As shown in the previous subsection, even though the dynamics and fixed point of geometrically local Lindbladians could be stable to noise on the analogue quantum simulator, a noisy quantum simulator cannot simulate observables perfectly but only to a noise-limited precision. We now adopt the perspective in Ref.
[
25
]
, and ask if there are family of stable Lindbladian problems where a reduction in the noise rate
\delta
results in classical algorithms requiring a superpolynomially longer time to achieve this noise-limited precision. To show that these problems exist (subject to the complexity assumption of BQP
\neq
BPP), we will leverage the quantum-circuit to 2D Lindbladian mapping presented in Lemma
7
.

We first consider the problem of rapidly mixing local observables in the Lindbladian fixed point in 2D. From Corollary
6.1
, it follows that, given
\delta>0
, as long as
\gamma^{-1}\leq O(\delta^{\alpha-1/(10(\kappa+1))})
for
\alpha>0
, the noise-limited precision in the estimated observable
\varepsilon\leq O(\delta^{5\alpha(\kappa+1)})\to 0
as
\delta\to 0
. Physically, this corresponds to the intuitively expected fact that a simulator with lower noise rate
\delta
can be used to solve problems which have a smaller decay rate
\gamma
(or equivalently those that take longer to reach their fixed point) without accumulating a large error. Now, using Lemma
7
we establish that there cannot exist a classical algorithm which for any
\alpha,\kappa>0
can compute the rapidly mixing local observable to a precision of
O\left(\delta^{5\alpha(\kappa+1)}\right)
in time
\text{poly}(\delta^{-1})
unless BQP=BPP.

Proposition
7
, repeated
.

A noisy analogue quantum simulator with noise rate
\delta
can solve Problem
1
, with parameters
\alpha,\kappa
, to a noise-limited precision
O(\delta^{5\alpha(\kappa+1)})
(which
\to 0
as
\delta\to 0
). Furthermore, there cannot exist a randomized classical algorithm with run-time
\text{poly}(\delta^{-1})
that can solve Problem
1
to the same precisions for every given
\alpha,\kappa
unless
BQP = BPP
.

Proof.

The estimate of the noise-limited precision and the quantum simulator run-time follows immediately from Corollary
6.1
of Proposition
6
. We now show the classical hardness of the sequence of problems defined in Problem
1
. Assume that such a classical algorithm did in fact exist — then, we can use it to simulate the outcome of measuring an output qubit in an arbitrary poly-depth quantum circuit. To see this, suppose we had a quantum circuit
\mathcal{C}
with architecture shown in Fig.
6
on
N
qubits with
R\leq O(N^{m})
rounds for some
m>0
. We can then use Lemma
7
to produce a corresponding 2D geometrically local Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}
and a rapidly mixing local observable
O
which satisfies the rapid-mixing condition (Eq.
4
) with
\kappa=1/3
and
\gamma^{-1}\leq O(N^{3(m+1)})
. Being able to simulate the local observable
O
to a precision of
O((NR)^{-1})=O(N^{-(m+1)})
would allow us to estimate, up to an
O(1)
additive error, the probability of the output qubit (which is arbitrarily chosen to be the first qubit) to be in 1, thus successfully simulating the quantum circuit.

Now, if we indeed had a classical algorithm that did satisfy the conditions in the proposition, then for a small
\delta>0
, we could use it simulate this Lindbladian for
N
being chosen as a function,
N_{\delta}
, of
\delta
to satisfy the constraint
\gamma^{-1}\leq O(\delta^{\alpha-1/(10(\kappa+1))})
i.e.

N^{3(m+1)}_{\delta}\leq O\left(\delta^{\alpha-3/40}\right),

and to satisfy the requirement that precision
\varepsilon=O(\delta^{\alpha(\kappa+1)})
be at-least
O((NR)^{-1})
, we impose that

\delta^{4\alpha/3}\leq O\left(N_{\delta}^{-(m+1)}\right).

We note that both of these requirements can be satisfied by using
\alpha=1/280
and
N_{\delta}=\Theta(\delta^{-1/(42(m+1))})
.

Thus, if there did exist a
\text{poly}(\delta^{-1})
classical algorithm to simulate the rapidly mixing Lindbladian problem, even with the constraint on
\gamma^{-1}
as stated in the proposition to ensure a vanishing noise-limited error as
\delta\to 0
, for any
\alpha,\kappa>0
, we could simulate the outcome of measurement of an output qubit in an arbitrary poly-depth quantum circuit, which would imply that BQP=BPP.
∎

Similarly, we can use Proposition
7
to establish a similar result for dynamics of geometrically local Lindbladians.

Corollary
7.1
, repeated
.

A noisy analogue quantum simulator with noise rate
\delta
can solve Problem
2
, with parameter
\alpha
, to a noise-limited precision
O(\delta^{5\alpha})
(which
\to 0
as
\delta\to 0
) in simulator run-time
O(\textnormal{poly}(\delta^{-1}))
and there cannot exist a
\textnormal{poly}(\delta^{-1})
randomized classical algorithm to estimate this local observable to the same precision for every given
\alpha>0
unless
BQP = BPP
.

Proof.

The estimate of the noise-limited precision and the quantum simulator run-time follows immediately from Proposition
5
. To show the classical hardness of the sequence of problems defined in Problem
2
, note that if such a classical algorithm did exist, then it could be used to simulate the sequence of fixed point problems in Proposition
7
to the corresponding noise-limited precision corresponding to
\alpha,\kappa>0
by simply choosing
t_{(\delta)}=\Theta(\gamma^{-(\kappa+1)}\log(\delta^{-1}))\leq O(\delta^{\alpha^{\prime}-1/10}\log(\delta^{-1}))
where
\alpha^{\prime}=(\kappa+1)\alpha
and thus implying that BQP = BPP.
∎

VI
A first experimental proposal: Non-thermality of Lindbladian fixed points

Conventionally, a large many-body system interacting with an environment is expected to thermalize to the environment’s temperature — consequently, the fixed point of the corresponding Lindbladian is expected to be a Gibb’s state at the environment’s temperature
[
56
]
. However, whether a precise microscopic open system model, described by a many-body Lindbladian, has a thermal fixed point remains much less clear largely due to the difficulty in classically numerically simulating Lindbladian fixed points
[
57
,
58
]
. It has been argued, either using the Keldysch path-integral formalism
[
59
,
60
,
61
]
or in exactly solvable models of gaussian fermions
[
62
]
, that local Lindbladians could have non-thermal fixed points — in particular, their correlation function structure as well as critical exponents cannot be explained by assuming them to be a thermal state. For instance, in 1D, there are Lindbladians which can support a long-range correlated fixed point (i.e. fixed points with correlation function that decay polynomially with the separation between the sites being correlated)
[
58
]
while it is known that Gibb’s states of 1D local Hamiltonians are necessarily short range correlated
[
63
,
64
]
. In 2 or higher dimensions, whether Lindbladian fixed points are thermal (i.e. are either the Gibb’s state of a geometrically local Hamiltonian or are in the same phase
[
52
]
as the Gibb’s state of a geometrically local Hamiltonian) are also less well understood.

Outside of solvable models of gaussian fermions
[
62
,
53
]
, to the best of our knowledge, there are not mathematically rigorous results or efficient numerical tools that precisely delineate under what conditions a many-body Lindbladian will have a thermal fixed point. This question could thus be potentially approached with an analog quantum simulator. However, we would then have to understand if noise in the simulator hampers our ability to answer this question at practically relevant system sizes. In the remainder of this section, we consider the 1D model with non-thermal fixed points proposed in Ref.
[
62
]
and provide numerical evidence of its noise robustness.

The model that we consider is shown in Fig.
7
(a). We consider a 1D system of
n
fermionic modes arranged with a Hamiltonian
H
given by

\displaystyle H=2h\sum_{x=1}^{n}n_{x}+\sum_{x=1}^{n-1}\big(a_{x}a^{\dagger}_{x+1}+\gamma a_{x}a_{x+1}+Un_{x}n_{x+1}+\text{h.c.}\big),

(28a)

where
a_{x}
is the annihilation operator for the fermion at site
x
and
n_{x}=a_{x}^{\dagger}a_{x}
is the corresponding number operator. Additionally, the chain of fermions is dissipated at the boundaries with jump operators
\sqrt{\Gamma_{L}}a_{1},\quad\sqrt{\Gamma_{R}}a_{n}
, where
\Gamma_{L}
and
\Gamma_{R}
are the dissipation rates at the left and right respectively. The full Lindbladian describing the system is thus given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(X)=-i[H,X]+\Gamma_{L}\mathcal{D}_{a_{1}}(X)+\Gamma_{R}\mathcal{D}_{a_{n}}(X).

(28b)

At
U=0
, this model is exactly solvable using the formalism in Ref.
[
53
]
, while for
U\neq 0
, the model is non-integrable. Furthermore, while we study this problem for fermions, it is equivalent to a 1D nearest neighbour spin model with boundary dissipation via the Jordan Wigner transformation.

At
U=0
and when
h\leq h_{c}=1-\gamma^{2}
, the steady state of this system is long range correlated and thus not thermal. This is shown in the first column in Figure
7
(b) — the steady-state covariance between fermionic occupation numbers,
\text{cov}(n_{x}n_{y})=\langle n_{x}n_{y}\rangle-\langle n_{x}\rangle\langle n_{y}\rangle
, shows a transition from the state being short-range correlated when
h>h_{c}
to being long-range correlated when
h\leq h_{c}
. Next, we consider adding simulator noise to this model — we do so by adding jump operators
\sqrt{\delta}a_{x},\sqrt{\delta}a_{x}^{\dagger}
at each site
x
, where
\delta
is the noise rate, and simulate instead the fixed point of the Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}_{\delta}
:

\mathcal{L}_{\delta}=\mathcal{L}+\delta\sum_{x=1}^{n}\big(\mathcal{D}_{a_{x}}+\mathcal{D}_{a_{x}^{\dagger}}\big).

In the absence of the boundary dissipation (i.e.
\Gamma_{L}=\Gamma_{R}=0
), this noise would have resulted the system being driven to the maximally mixed state in its steady state. However, in the presence of the boundary dissipation (i.e.
\Gamma_{L},\Gamma_{R}\neq 0
), we find that the covariance function measured on the simulator can still show signature of a long-range phase at
\delta\sim 0.5\%-1\%
at large system sizes (
n\sim 160
) as shown in Fig.
7
(b). Specifically, we observe that the quantum simulator can show significant signatures of long-range correlations when the system is deep inside the long-range correlated phase (
h=0.4-0.6
in Fig.
7
(b)), while near the phase transition point (
h=h_{c}
), the presence of noise in the simulator destroys the long-range correlations. Nevertheless, these simulations indicate that the noisy simulator would be able to detect non-thermality in the steady state for system sizes practically relevant for current experiments, but make a noise-rate limited error in detecting the point at which the phase transition occurs.

For
U\neq 0
, the Lindbladian does not remain integrable and the parameter regimes in which the Lindbladian fixed point is non-thermal have not been precisely delineated. Based on the noise analysis for
U=0
discussed above, we conjecture that a noisy simulator should be able to detect the existence of a non-thermal phase in the model and approximate, to a noise-rate limited precision, the model parameters (
h,U_{0},\gamma
) at which this phase transition occurs. We remark that our theoretical result on the stability of fixed point (Proposition
6
) does not directly apply to this problem and rigorously ensure that a noisy simulator can detect non-thermality in Lindbladian fixed points — we leave a thorough theoretical and numerical investigation of this problem for future work.

…
n

\sqrt{\Gamma_{R}}a_{n}

\sqrt{\delta}a^{\dagger}_{1}
\sqrt{\delta}a_{2}
\sqrt{\delta}a^{\dagger}_{2}
\sqrt{\delta}a_{3}
\sqrt{\delta}a^{\dagger}_{3}
\sqrt{\delta}a_{n}
\sqrt{\delta}a^{\dagger}_{n}
(a)
(b)

Figure 7:
(a) Schematic of the open 1D fermion chain model described by Eq.
28
. Solid lines indicate hamiltonian (smooth) and dissipative (squiggly) terms in target model, and dotted lines indicate noise. (b) Numerically computed occupation covariance for pairs of fermions in the steady state of the noisy fermion chain model with
\gamma=0.5,\Gamma_{R},\Gamma_{L}=0.5
. For each pair
x,y
of fermions, we plot
{|\text{cov}(n_{x}n_{y})|}
. In the noiseless case (
\delta=0
), there is a phase change from short-range to long-range correlations as
h
is brought lower than
h_{c}=1-\gamma^{2}=0.75
. When depolarizing noise is added (
\delta>0
), the phase transition point is effectively “delayed” to lower values of
h
.

VII
Conclusion

In conclusion, we have provided an analysis of analogue quantum simulation of physically motivated open quantum system simulation problems. Our analysis developed tools to study both a noiseless quantum simulator as well as a noisy quantum simulator and provided rigorous accuracy guarantees on the observables being measured on the quantum simulator. Furthermore, we also provided complexity theoretic evidence of classical hardness of the physically motivated problems that we considered. Our results provide theoretical evidence for the possibility of using near-term quantum devices for solving physically interesting and classically hard open system simulation problems, while remaining stable to noise. Our paper also introduces several new technical results that could be of independent interest — the accuracy and stability guarantees that we develop in this paper were based on developing a mathematically rigorous adiabatic elimination analysis that could account for Lieb-Robinson bounds, and the classical hardness results were built on a quantum circuit to 2D Lindbladian encoding together with its convergence analysis.

Another promising direction investigating open system simulation problems on analog simulators can be inspired by Ref.
[
65
]
, in which a protocol is proposed for verifying quantum advantage on analog hamiltonian simulators and is shown to have some robustness to noise in the simulation. This type of verification protocol is useful in the scenario that the quantum simulator is not implementing the claimed computation - either due to uncalibrated noise or if the operator of the simulator is adversarial to the party attempting to establish the presence of quantum advantage. In our work we make the physics-oriented assumption that the simulator implements the simulation as defined and that errors arise from noise that is well calibrated and known in advance. In future work, we hope to determine if the physically-motivated universal quantum simulation problem we consider can also be efficiently verified in the same sense as Ref.
[
65
]
.

Our work leaves several important theoretical questions open — most importantly, can we develop certifiable analogue simulation protocols for the analogue simulation for the fixed point of Lindbladians that are not rapidly mixing, and are these protocols stable to errors? A possible approach to this problem, which we leave for future work, is to identify a set of reasonable assumptions on the spectrum of Lindbladians, that may not be rapid mixing, but which still allow for analytical results related to their stability. An alternative could be studying Lindbladians showing dissipative phase transitions numerically near the phase transition, and understand if (and which) local observables are stable.

Furthermore, another direction would be extending the classical hardness results to 1D geometrically local Lindbladians by encoding a quantum circuit in the fixed point of such a Lindbladian, thereby making the case for quantum advantage in simpler (i.e. 1D) experimental setups. This should in principle be possible by adapting the techniques developed for the corresponding result that encodes a quantum circuit to the ground state of a 1D Hamiltonian
[
55
]
. Furthermore, extending classical hardness results to translationally invariant Lindbladians, along the lines of similar results in Hamiltonian ground states
[
66
]
, also remain open.

Appendix A
Detailed proofs from section
IV

Lemma
1
, repeated
.

For any
t>0
, the remainder
\mathcal{R}_{\omega}(t)=\frac{d}{dt}\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\rho_{\omega}(t))-\omega^{2}\mathcal{L}\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\rho_{\omega}(t))
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\omega}(t)

\displaystyle=\omega^{2}\sum_{\alpha}e^{-2t}q_{\alpha}+\omega^{4}\sum_{j\in\{1,2\}}\sum_{\alpha}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-2(t-s)}\mathcal{Q}^{(j)}_{\alpha,H_{\textnormal{sys}}}(s)ds

\displaystyle\quad+\omega^{4}\sum_{j\in\{3,4\}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-2(t-s)}Q_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}^{(j)}(s)ds.

(29a)

where

\displaystyle q_{\alpha}=-\mathcal{D}_{L_{\alpha}}(\rho(0)),

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,h}^{(1)}(t)=-\frac{1}{\omega}[L_{\alpha}^{\dagger},[h,\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\alpha}\rho_{\omega}(t))]]+\textnormal{h.c.}

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,h}^{(2)}(t)=-\frac{i}{2}[L_{\alpha}^{\dagger},L_{\alpha}[h,\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\rho_{\omega}(t))]]+\textnormal{h.c.}.

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}^{(3)}(t)=\frac{i}{\omega}\sum_{u\in\{+,-\}}\mathcal{D}_{L_{\alpha}}\big([L_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{(u)},\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{(\bar{u})}\rho_{\omega}(t))]\big),

\displaystyle\text{If }\alpha=\alpha^{\prime},\mathcal{Q}^{(4)}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(t)=\frac{2}{\omega^{2}}(\mathcal{D}_{L_{\alpha}^{\dagger}}-\mathcal{D}_{L_{\alpha}})(\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(n_{\alpha}\rho_{\omega}(t))),

\displaystyle\text{If }\alpha\neq\alpha^{\prime},\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}^{(4)}(t)=-\frac{1}{\omega^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u,u^{\prime}\\
\in\{+,-\}\end{subarray}}[L_{\alpha}^{(u)},[L_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{(u^{\prime})},\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\alpha}^{({\bar{u}})}\sigma_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{(\bar{u}^{\prime})}\rho_{\omega}(t))]].

Proof.

In order to find
\mathcal{R}_{\omega}(t)
, we start from the equations of motion for
\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}({\rho_{\omega}}(t))
and
\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\alpha}\rho_{\omega}(t))
as given in Eqs.
8
a,b. We first integrate Eq.
8
b to obtain

\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\alpha}\rho_{\omega}(t))=\int_{0}^{t}e^{-2(t-s)}\bigg(-i\omega^{2}[H_{\text{sys}},\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\alpha}\rho_{\omega}(s))]-i\omega L_{\alpha}\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\rho_{\omega}(s))+\omega\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}}E_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(s)\bigg)ds,

(30)

where we have used the fact that
\sigma_{\alpha}\rho_{\omega}(0)=0
, since the ancillae are initialized in
\ket{0}
.
Next we note that, from integration by parts and the expression for
\frac{d}{dt}\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\rho_{\omega}(t))
in Eq.
8
a, it follows that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}e^{-2(t-s)}L_{\alpha}\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\rho_{\omega}(s))ds

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}L_{\alpha}\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\rho_{\omega}(t))-\frac{e^{-2t}}{2}L_{\alpha}\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\rho_{\omega}(0))-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-2(t-s)}L_{\alpha}\frac{d}{ds}\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\rho_{\omega}(s))ds,

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}L_{\alpha}\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\rho_{\omega}(t))-\frac{e^{-2t}}{2}L_{\alpha}\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\rho_{\omega}(0))-\frac{i\omega^{2}}{2}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-2(t-s)}L_{\alpha}[H_{\text{sys}},\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\rho_{\omega}(s))]ds+\frac{i\omega}{2}\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-2(t-s)}F_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(s)ds,

(31)

where

F_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(s)=\sum_{u\in\{+,-\}}L_{\alpha}[L_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{(u)},\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{(\bar{u})}\rho_{\omega}(s))].

(32)

From Eqs.
30
and
31
, we now obtain that

\displaystyle\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\alpha}\rho_{\omega}(t))=-\frac{i\omega}{2}L_{\alpha}\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\rho_{\omega}(t))+\frac{i\omega}{2}e^{-2t}L_{\alpha}\rho(0)

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{0}^{t}e^{-2(t-s)}\Bigg(-i\omega^{2}[H_{\text{sys}},\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\alpha}\rho_{\omega}(s)))]-\frac{\omega^{3}}{2}L_{\alpha}[H_{\text{sys}},\text{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\rho_{\omega}(s))]+\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}}\bigg(\omega{E_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(s)}+\frac{\omega^{2}}{2}{F_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(s)}\bigg)\Bigg)ds.

(33)

We now consider the remainder
\mathcal{R}_{\omega}(t)
— using the definition of the remainder (Eq.
10
) together with Eq.
8
a, we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\omega}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\rho_{\omega}(t))-\omega^{2}\mathcal{L}\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\rho_{\omega}(t))=\sum_{\alpha}\bigg(\left(-i\omega[L_{\alpha}^{\dagger},\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\alpha}{\rho}_{\omega}(t))]+\text{h.c}\right)-\omega^{2}\mathcal{D}_{L_{\alpha}}\textnormal{Tr}_{A}(\rho_{\omega}(t))\bigg).

(34)

It follows from Eq.
33
that

\displaystyle-i\omega[L_{\alpha}^{\dagger},\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\alpha}\rho_{\omega}(t))]=-\frac{\omega^{2}}{2}[L_{\alpha}^{\dagger},L_{\alpha}\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\rho_{\omega}(t))]+\frac{\omega^{2}}{2}e^{-2t}[L_{\alpha}^{\dagger},L_{\alpha}\rho(0)]+\int_{0}^{t}e^{2(t-s)}\bigg(-\omega^{3}[L_{\alpha}^{\dagger},[H_{\text{sys}},\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\alpha}\rho_{\omega}(t))]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-i\frac{\omega^{4}}{2}[L_{\alpha}^{\dagger},L_{\alpha}[H_{\text{sys}},\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\rho_{\omega}(s))]]-i\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}}\bigg(\omega^{2}[L_{\alpha}^{\dagger},E_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(s)]+\frac{\omega^{3}}{2}[L_{\alpha}^{\dagger},F_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(s)]\bigg)\bigg)ds,

(35)

Using Eqs.
34
and
35
a, b, we note that
-[L_{\alpha}^{\dagger},L\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\rho_{\omega}(s))]/2+\text{h.c}=\mathcal{D}_{L_{\alpha}}
and so arrive at

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\omega}(t)=\sum_{\alpha}\left(\omega^{2}e^{-2t}q_{\alpha}+\omega^{4}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-2(t-s)}\left({\mathcal{Q}^{(1)}_{\alpha,H_{\textnormal{sys}}}(s)}+\omega^{4}\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{\alpha,H_{\textnormal{sys}}}(s)\right)ds\right)+\omega^{4}\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-2(t-s)}\big({\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(s)}+\mathcal{Q}^{(4)}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(s)\big)ds,

(36)

where

\displaystyle q_{\alpha}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}[L_{\alpha}^{\dagger},L_{\alpha}\rho(0)]+\text{h.c.},

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{(1)}_{\alpha,H_{\textnormal{sys}}}(t)

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{\omega}\left[L_{\alpha}^{\dagger},[H_{\text{sys}},\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\alpha}\rho_{\omega}(t))]\right]+\text{h.c.},

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{(2)}_{\alpha,H_{\textnormal{sys}}}(t)

\displaystyle:=-\frac{i}{2}[L_{\alpha}^{\dagger},L_{\alpha}[H_{\text{sys}},\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\rho_{\omega}(t))]]+\text{h.c.},

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{(3)}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(t)

\displaystyle:=\frac{-i}{2\omega}[L_{\alpha}^{\dagger},F_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(t)]+\text{h.c},

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{(4)}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(t)

\displaystyle:=-\frac{i}{\omega^{2}}[L_{\alpha}^{\dagger},E_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}(t)]+\text{h.c.}.

(37)

The expressions for
q_{\alpha}
,
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,H_{sys}}^{(1)}(t)
,
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,H_{sys}}^{(2)}(t)
,
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}^{(3)}(t)
, and
\mathcal{Q}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}^{(4)}(t)
can be seen to be the same as those provided in the lemma statement.
∎

Lemma
2
, repeated
.

Suppose
\rho_{\omega}(t)
is the joint state of the system and the ancilla qubits with the ancilla qubits initially being in state
\ket{0}
, then for all
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}

\displaystyle{|\hskip-1.084pt|\sigma_{\alpha}\rho_{\omega}(t)|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{1}\leq\frac{\omega}{2}\text{ and }{|\hskip-1.084pt|\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\alpha}^{\dagger}\sigma_{\alpha^{\prime}}\rho_{\omega}(t))|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{1},{|\hskip-1.084pt|\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\alpha}\sigma_{\alpha^{\prime}}\rho_{\omega}(t))|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{1}\leq\frac{\omega^{2}}{4}.

Proof.

The proof of the bound on
{|\hskip-1.084pt|\sigma_{\alpha}\rho_{\omega}(t)|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{1}
is provided after the statement of Lemma
2
in Subsection
IV.1
. Here we bound
{|\hskip-1.084pt|\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\alpha}^{\dagger}\sigma_{\alpha^{\prime}}\rho_{\omega}(t))|\hskip-1.084pt|}
and
{|\hskip-1.084pt|\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\alpha}\sigma_{\alpha^{\prime}}\rho_{\omega}(t))|\hskip-1.084pt|}
for any
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}
. It is convenient to define the “Heisenberg-like” picture of any operator or superoperator
O
to be
O(t):=\mathcal{E}_{\omega}^{-1}(t,0)O\mathcal{E}_{\omega}(t,0)
. Accordingly we may write in vectorized form that, for
u\in\{-,+\}
,

\frac{d}{dt}\left(\sigma_{\alpha,u}^{(u)}(t)\sigma_{\alpha^{\prime},l}(t)\right)=[\sigma_{\alpha,u}^{(u)}(t)\sigma_{\alpha^{\prime},l}(t),\mathcal{L_{\omega}}(t)]=-4\sigma_{\alpha,u}^{(u)}(t)-i\omega L_{\alpha^{\prime},l}(t)\sigma_{\alpha^{\prime},l}^{z}(t)\sigma_{\alpha,u}^{(u)}(t)+ui\omega L_{\alpha,u}^{(u)}(t)\sigma_{\alpha,u}^{z}(t)\sigma_{\alpha^{\prime},l}(t),

where we interpret the subscripts as
-=l,+=r
and
\sigma_{\alpha}^{z}:=[\sigma_{\alpha},\sigma_{\alpha}^{\dagger}]
is the pauli-Z operator acting on ancilla qubit
\alpha
. Integrating the above equation, we obtain a relation between
\sigma_{\alpha,u}^{(u)}(t)\sigma_{\alpha,l}(t)
and
\sigma_{\alpha,u}^{(u)}\sigma_{\alpha,l}
,

\sigma_{\alpha,u}^{(u)}(t)\sigma_{\alpha,l}(t)=e^{-4t}\sigma_{\alpha,u}^{(u)}\sigma_{\alpha^{\prime},l}+i\omega\int_{0}^{t}e^{-4(t-t^{\prime})}\left(-L_{\alpha^{\prime},l}(t^{\prime})\sigma_{\alpha^{\prime},l}^{z}(t^{\prime})\sigma_{\alpha,u}^{(u)}(t^{\prime})+uL_{\alpha,u}^{(u)}(t^{\prime})\sigma_{\alpha,u}^{z}(t^{\prime})\sigma_{\alpha^{\prime},l}(t^{\prime})\right)dt^{\prime}.

We express the bound on
{|\hskip-1.084pt|\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\alpha}^{(u)}\sigma_{\alpha^{\prime}}\rho_{\omega}(t))|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{1}
in vectorized form using the above equation as

\displaystyle{|\hskip-1.084pt|\llangle{\text{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}}|\sigma_{\alpha,l}^{(u)}\sigma_{\alpha^{\prime},l}|{\rho_{\omega}(t)}\rrangle|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{1}={|\hskip-1.084pt|\llangle{\text{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}}|\mathcal{E}_{\omega}(t,0)\sigma_{\alpha,u}^{(u)}(t)\sigma_{\alpha,l}(t)|{\rho(0)}\rrangle|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{1}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\omega\int_{0}^{t}e^{-4(t-t^{\prime})}\left({|\hskip-1.084pt|\llangle{\text{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}}|\mathcal{E}_{\omega}(t,0)L_{\alpha^{\prime},l}(t^{\prime})\sigma_{\alpha^{\prime},l}^{z}(t^{\prime})\sigma_{\alpha,u}^{(u)}(t^{\prime})|{\rho(0)}\rrangle|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{1}+{|\hskip-1.084pt|\llangle{\text{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}}|\mathcal{E}_{\omega}(t,0)L_{\alpha,u}^{(u)}(t^{\prime})\sigma_{\alpha,u}^{z}(t^{\prime})\sigma_{\alpha^{\prime},l}(t^{\prime})|{\rho(0)}\rrangle|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{1}\right)dt^{\prime},

(38)

where we have used the assumption that
\rho(0)
is initialized with all ancilla in vacuum to apply
\sigma_{\alpha^{\prime},l}|{\rho(0)}\rrangle=0
. Using the bound
{|\hskip-1.084pt|\sigma_{\alpha}\rho_{\omega}(t^{\prime})|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{1}\leq\omega/2
for any
\alpha
from the first part of Lemma
2
in conjunction with the contracting effect of channels on the 1-norm, we have
{|\hskip-1.084pt|\sigma_{\alpha,l}(t)|{\rho(0)}\rrangle|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{1},{|\hskip-1.084pt|\sigma_{\alpha,r}^{\dagger}(t)|{\rho(0)}\rrangle|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{1}\leq\omega/2
for any
\alpha
and
t\geq 0
. Inserting these bounds into the above equation and applying the assumption
{|\hskip-1.084pt|L_{\alpha}|\hskip-1.084pt|}\leq 1
, we find

\displaystyle{|\hskip-1.084pt|\textnormal{Tr}_{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\alpha}^{(u)}\sigma_{\alpha}\rho(t))|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{1}\leq\omega\int_{0}^{t}e^{-4(t-t^{\prime})}\left(\frac{\omega}{2}+\frac{\omega}{2}\right)dt^{\prime}\leq\frac{\omega^{2}}{4}.

(39)

The lemma statement is obtained by either setting
u=-
or
u=+
.
∎

Appendix B
Detailed proofs of lemmas from section
IV.2

Throughout this appendix, we will focus on geometrically local Lindbladians introduced in section
IV.2
. These will be Lindbladians of the form

\mathcal{L}=\sum_{\alpha}\mathcal{L}_{\alpha},

where we will assume that
\mathcal{L}_{\alpha}(X)=-i[h_{\alpha},X]+\mathcal{D}_{L_{\alpha}}(X)
for operators
h_{\alpha},L_{\alpha}
supported on
S_{\alpha}
. Furthermore, we will assume that diameter
\text{diam}(S_{\alpha})\leq a
for all
\alpha
, and the degree
\partial(S_{\alpha})=\{\alpha^{\prime}:S_{\alpha}\cap S_{\alpha^{\prime}}=\emptyset\}\leq\mathcal{Z}
for all
\alpha
. As was mentioned in section
IV.2
, a key ingredient in our analysis is the Lieb-Robinson bound from Ref.
[
44
]
.

Lemma
3
, repeated
.

Suppose
\mathcal{K}
is a superoperator supported on region
S_{\mathcal{K}}
and satisfies
\mathcal{K}(I)=0
, and
O
is a local observable, with
{|\hskip-1.084pt|O|\hskip-1.084pt|}\leq 1
, supported on region
S_{O}
, then

\displaystyle{|\hskip-1.084pt|\mathcal{K}e^{\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}t}(O)|\hskip-1.084pt|}\leq\eta_{S_{O}}{|\hskip-1.084pt|\mathcal{K}|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{cb,\infty\to\infty}\exp\bigg(4e\mathcal{Z}t-\frac{d({S_{\mathcal{K}},S_{O}})}{a}\bigg).

An immediate consequence of this lemma is the following modified “2-point” Lieb-Robinson bound.

Lemma
4
, repeated
.

\mathcal{K}_{X}
and
\mathcal{K}_{Y}
are superoperators supported on regions
X
and
Y
such that
\mathcal{K}_{X}(I)=\mathcal{K}_{Y}(I)=0
and
\textnormal{diam}(X),\textnormal{diam}(Y)\leq a
, then for any local observable
O
, with
{|\hskip-1.084pt|O|\hskip-1.084pt|}\leq 1
, supported on region
S_{O}

\displaystyle{|\hskip-1.084pt|\mathcal{K}_{X}\mathcal{K}_{Y}e^{\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}t}(O)|\hskip-1.084pt|}\leq e\eta_{S_{O}}{|\hskip-1.084pt|\mathcal{K}_{X}|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{cb,\infty\to\infty}{|\hskip-1.084pt|\mathcal{K}_{Y}|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{cb,\infty\to\infty}\exp\bigg(4e\mathcal{Z}t-\frac{1}{2a}(d({X,S_{O}})+d({Y,S_{O}}))\bigg).

Proof.

Note that

\displaystyle{|\hskip-1.084pt|\mathcal{K}_{X}\mathcal{K}_{Y}e^{\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}t}(O)|\hskip-1.084pt|}

\displaystyle\leq{|\hskip-1.084pt|\mathcal{K}_{X}|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{cb,\infty\to\infty}{|\hskip-1.084pt|\mathcal{K}_{Y}e^{\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}t}(O)|\hskip-1.084pt|}\leq\frac{\partial(S_{O})}{\mathcal{Z}}{|\hskip-1.084pt|\mathcal{K}_{X}|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{cb,\infty\to\infty}{|\hskip-1.084pt|\mathcal{K}_{Y}|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{cb,\infty\to\infty}\exp\bigg(4e\mathcal{Z}t-\frac{1}{a}d(Y,S_{O})\bigg).

(40)

Next, consider the case
X\cap Y=\emptyset
, then
\mathcal{K}_{X}\mathcal{K}_{Y}=\mathcal{K}_{Y}\mathcal{K}_{X}
. In this case, the above equation holds with
X
and
Y
swapped i.e.

\displaystyle{|\hskip-1.084pt|\mathcal{K}_{X}\mathcal{K}_{Y}e^{\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}t}(O)|\hskip-1.084pt|}\leq\frac{\partial(S_{O})}{\mathcal{Z}}{|\hskip-1.084pt|\mathcal{K}_{X}|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{cb,\infty\to\infty}{|\hskip-1.084pt|\mathcal{K}_{Y}|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{cb,\infty\to\infty}\exp\bigg(4e\mathcal{Z}t-\frac{1}{a}d(X,S_{O})\bigg).

(41)

Multiplying Eqs.
40
with
41
, we obtain that

\displaystyle{|\hskip-1.084pt|\mathcal{K}_{X}\mathcal{K}_{Y}e^{\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}t}(O)|\hskip-1.084pt|}\leq\frac{\partial(S_{O})}{\mathcal{Z}}{|\hskip-1.084pt|\mathcal{K}_{X}|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{cb,\infty\to\infty}{|\hskip-1.084pt|\mathcal{K}_{Y}|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{cb,\infty\to\infty}\exp\bigg(4e\mathcal{Z}t-\frac{1}{2a}\big(d(X,S_{O})+d(Y,S_{O})\big)\bigg)

(42)

Consider now the case when
X\cap Y\neq\emptyset
. In this case, we can treat
\mathcal{K}_{X}\mathcal{K}_{Y}
as a superoperator supported on
X\cup Y
. From the Lieb-Robinson bounds, it then follows that

\displaystyle{|\hskip-1.084pt|\mathcal{K}_{X}\mathcal{K}_{Y}e^{\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}t}(O)|\hskip-1.084pt|}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\partial(S_{O})}{\mathcal{Z}}{|\hskip-1.084pt|\mathcal{K}_{X}\mathcal{K}_{Y}|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{cb,\infty\to\infty}\exp\bigg(4e\mathcal{Z}t-\frac{1}{a}d(X\cup Y,S_{O})\bigg).

(43)

We note that
{|\hskip-1.084pt|\mathcal{K}_{X}\mathcal{K}_{Y}|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{cb,\infty\to\infty}\leq{|\hskip-1.084pt|\mathcal{K}_{X}|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{cb,\infty\to\infty}{|\hskip-1.084pt|\mathcal{K}_{Y}|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{cb,\infty\to\infty}
. Next, we note that

d(X\cup Y,S_{O})=\min(d(X,S_{O}),d(Y,S_{O})=\min(d(x_{0},S_{O}),d(y_{0},S_{O})),

where
x_{0}=\textnormal{argmin}_{x\in X}d(x,S_{O})
and
y_{0}=\textnormal{argmin}_{y\in Y}d(y,S_{O})
. Since
\textnormal{diam}(X),\textnormal{diam}(Y)\leq a
, we have
d(x_{0},y_{0})\leq a
and so
\min(d(x_{0},S_{O}),d(y_{0},S_{O}))\geq d(x_{0},S_{O})-d(x_{0},y_{0})=d(X,S_{0})-a
. By the same logic we also have
\min(d(x_{0},S_{O}),d(y_{0},S_{O}))\geq d(Y,S_{0})-a
and consequently we have that

d(X\cup Y,S_{O})\geq\frac{1}{2}(d(X(S_{O}))+d(Y(S_{O})))-a.

Therefore, from Eq.
43
, we obtain that when
X\cap Y\neq\emptyset
,

\displaystyle{|\hskip-1.084pt|\mathcal{K}_{X}\mathcal{K}_{Y}e^{\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}t}(O)|\hskip-1.084pt|}\leq\frac{e\partial(S_{O})}{\mathcal{Z}}{|\hskip-1.084pt|\mathcal{K}_{X}|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{cb,\infty\to\infty}{|\hskip-1.084pt|\mathcal{K}_{Y}|\hskip-1.084pt|}_{cb,\infty\to\infty}\exp\bigg(4e\mathcal{Z}t-\frac{1}{2a}(d(X,S_{O})+d(Y,S_{O}))\bigg).

(44)

From Eqs.
42
and
44
, the lemma statement follows.
∎

An upper bound on the sum of an exponential function over a
d-
dimensional lattice is a useful technical result.

Lemma 12
.

Suppose
\tilde{S}_{\alpha}
, for
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
, are subsets of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
which satisfy that for some
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}>0
,
\partial_{\tilde{S}_{\alpha}}={|\{\alpha^{\prime}:\tilde{S}_{\alpha}\cap\tilde{S}_{\alpha^{\prime}}\neq\emptyset\}|}\leq\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}
for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
. Suppose
S\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with
{|S|}<\infty
is a fixed subset of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. For any
k\in\{1,2\dots\}
,
m\in\{0,1\}
and
T,\lambda,x\geq 0
, define

\xi^{(m,k)}_{\lambda,x}(T)=\sum_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\dots\alpha_{k}}d_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\dots\alpha_{k}}^{m}\min\bigg(x\exp\bigg(T-\frac{1}{\lambda}d_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\dots\alpha_{k}}\bigg),1\bigg),

where
d_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\dots\alpha_{k}}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}d(S,\tilde{S}_{\alpha_{j}})
. Then,

\xi^{(m,k)}_{\lambda,x}(T)\leq\max(x,1)^{k}k^{m}\big(\nu^{(0)}(\lambda,T)\big)^{k-m}\big(\nu^{(1)}(\lambda,T)\big)^{m},

where,
\nu^{(m)}(\lambda,T)
are piecewise continuous non-decreasing functions of
\lambda
(for a fixed
T
) and
T
(for a fixed
\lambda
) which only depend on
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}
and
d
. Furthermore,

\nu^{(m)}(\lambda,T)\leq O((\lambda T)^{m+d})\text{ as }\lambda,T\to\infty\text{ and }\nu^{(m)}(\lambda,0)\leq O(\lambda^{m+d})\text{ as }\lambda\to\infty.

Proof.

We can reduce the multiple summation to a single summation — and important fact that we will use here is that for
0<x_{1},x_{2}\dots x_{k}\leq 1
and
A>1
,

\displaystyle\min(Ax_{1}x_{2}\dots x_{k},1)\leq\min(Ax_{1},1)\min(Ax_{2},1)\dots\prod_{i=1}^{k}\min(Ax_{i},1).

(45)

To see that Eq.
45
is true, consider two cases — if
Ax_{1}x_{2}\dots x_{k}\geq 1
, then for any
i\in[1:k]
,

Ax_{i}\geq\frac{1}{x_{1}\dots x_{i-1}x_{i+1}\dots x_{k}}\geq 1\implies\min(Ax_{i},1)=1

and hence satisfies Eq.
45
. On the other hand if
Ax_{1}x_{2}\dots x_{k}\leq 1
, then we again have that

\min(Ax_{1}x_{2}\dots x_{k},1)=Ax_{1}x_{2}\dots x_{k}\leq\prod_{i\in S}Ax_{i}\text{ for any }S\subseteq[1:k]\implies\min(Ax_{1}x_{2}\dots x_{k},1)\leq\prod_{i=1}^{k}\min(Ax_{i},1).

where we have used that
0<x_{i}\leq 1
and
A>1
.
Now, noting that for any
x,y>0
,
\min(y,x)\leq\max(x,1)\min(y,1)
. Using this together with Eq.
45
, we obtain that,

\displaystyle\xi^{(0,k)}_{\lambda,x}(T)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\dots\alpha_{k}}\min\bigg(x\exp\bigg(T-\frac{1}{\lambda}d_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\dots\alpha_{k}}\bigg),1\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\max(x,1)\sum_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\dots\alpha_{k}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\min\bigg(\exp\bigg(T-\frac{1}{\lambda}d(\tilde{S}_{\alpha_{i}},S)\bigg),1\bigg),

\displaystyle\leq\max(x,1)^{k}\bigg(\sum_{\alpha}\min\bigg(\exp\bigg(T-\frac{1}{\lambda}d(\tilde{S}_{\alpha},S)\bigg),1\bigg)\bigg)^{k}.

(46a)

and

\displaystyle\xi^{(1,k)}_{\lambda,x}(T)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\dots\alpha_{k}}d_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\dots\alpha_{k}}\min\bigg(\exp\bigg(T-\frac{1}{\lambda}d_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\dots\alpha_{k}}\bigg),x\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\max(x,1)\sum_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\dots\alpha_{k}}\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{k}d(\tilde{S}_{\alpha_{i}},S)\bigg)\prod_{i=1}^{k}\min\bigg(\exp\bigg(T-\frac{1}{\lambda}d(\tilde{S}_{\alpha_{i}},S)\bigg),1\bigg),

\displaystyle=k\max(x,1)\sum_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\dots\alpha_{k}}d(\tilde{S}_{\alpha_{1}},S)\prod_{i=1}^{k}\min\bigg(\exp\bigg(T-\frac{1}{\lambda}d(\tilde{S}_{\alpha_{i}},S)\bigg),1\bigg),

\displaystyle\leq k\max(x,1)^{k}\bigg(\sum_{\alpha}\min\bigg(\exp\bigg(T-\frac{1}{\lambda}d(\tilde{S}_{\alpha},S)\bigg),1\bigg)\bigg)^{k-1}\sum_{\alpha}d(\tilde{S}_{\alpha},S)\min\bigg(\exp\bigg(T-\frac{1}{\lambda}d(\tilde{S}_{\alpha},S)\bigg),1\bigg).

(46b)

Having reduced the multiple summation to a single summation, we re-express the single summation with respect to
\alpha
with a summation with respect to the grid distances. In particular, we note that
